  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.9, F32.9, F50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just been… really all over the place lately. Like, some weeks I barely touch dinner, I’ll make something or sit down with it and then just kind of pick at it and give up. And then other nights it’s the total opposite—I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, just sort of one thing after another.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because it doesn’t feel normal for me. It’s not even like I’m planning it, it’s just this weird swing between not wanting anything and then suddenly eating late at night like I can’t settle. That’s the thing that made me think, okay, maybe I should actually talk to someone instead of just pretending it’ll even out on its own.\n\nDoctor: After those nights when you eat a lot, do you ever make yourself vomit?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not like intentionally making myself throw up or anything. Afterward I usually feel gross and kind of ashamed, and sometimes my stomach feels too full, like I overdid it, but I don’t go to the bathroom to make myself vomit. I mostly just sit with that awful feeling and tell myself I won’t do it again, and then sometimes it happens again anyway.\n\nDoctor: Are you very afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “very afraid,” exactly. I mean, I don’t love the idea of gaining weight—who does, really—but it’s not like I’m constantly weighing myself or panicking over every bite because I think I’ll get fat.\n\nIt’s more that after I eat a lot, I feel uncomfortable and kind of upset with myself, and then I start thinking, “Why did I do that?” But I’m not sure it’s specifically about weight gain. It’s more like feeling out of control, or like I did something I shouldn’t have. So… no, I don’t think I’m very afraid of gaining weight, not in a way that’s driving everything.\n\nDoctor: When those late-night eating episodes happen, do you feel unable to stop eating once you’ve started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it does feel that way, but it’s hard to explain because it’s not always like I’m starving or anything. It’s more like once I’m standing there in the kitchen, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll have a few crackers or something sweet, and then it kind of turns into another thing, and then another thing. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough,” but I don’t really walk away right away.\n\nAnd that’s what feels so weird compared to the weeks where I barely touch dinner. I’ll sit down with food and just pick at it, like I can’t be bothered or it doesn’t appeal to me, and then later there are these nights where I’m eating in this restless way. I don’t know if it’s that I physically can’t stop, but it feels like I don’t stop when I mean to. Then afterward I feel embarrassed, like, why was I doing that alone in the kitchen?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have been. Not every single minute, and it’s not always dramatic, but I get these stretches where everything in the house just feels kind of gray and heavy. Like I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and nothing terrible has even happened that day, but I feel empty, like the air got sucked out of me or something.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside I probably look like I’m just sitting there, maybe drinking coffee or staring at the counter, but inside it feels… flat. Heavy. Like there’s this sadness settled over everything, even ordinary things. Sometimes I’ll think, “What is wrong with me? Nothing happened today,” and that almost makes it worse because I can’t point to a reason. It’s just there.\n\nDoctor: When those late-night episodes happen, do you eat what most people would consider a large amount of food for that period of time?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know how “large” it would look to someone else, exactly. It’s not like I’m sitting down with a whole prepared meal or anything, but it can add up because I’ll be standing there in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep, and it starts with crackers, then maybe something sweet, then I go back for more crackers, and then I’m opening another cabinet.\n\nSo in that period of time, yeah, sometimes it feels like more than I meant to eat, especially because it’s late and I already had the chance to eat dinner earlier. But it’s also weird because some weeks I barely touch dinner at all, like I’ll just pick at it and not really want it, and then other nights I’m eating all this snack food in the kitchen. That back-and-forth is really what pushed me to come in, honestly—my eating feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you barely touch dinner, do you feel like you’ve lost your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… during those weeks, it does feel like my appetite just kind of drops off. I’ll make dinner or sit down with everyone, and then when the plate is in front of me, I just don’t really want it. I’ll take a few bites, move things around, maybe tell myself I should eat more, but it’s like my body isn’t interested.\n\nAnd that’s part of what’s been so confusing, because it’s not consistent. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like food just doesn’t appeal to me much, and then other nights I’m up late after everyone’s gone to bed, standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. That back-and-forth is really what made me feel like something was off enough to come talk about it. My eating just feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I never enjoy anything at all, but things I used to actually look forward to feel weirdly hard to start now. Like my book club—I used to pick out the book and think about what I wanted to say, and now even reading the chapters feels like this assignment I’m behind on. I’ll see the book sitting there and just feel tired before I even open it.\n\nAnd cooking used to be something I could get absorbed in, like planning a meal or trying a new recipe, but lately it feels more like another chore in the line of chores. Same with going to galleries. I used to love wandering around and looking at everything slowly, and now I keep putting it off, even when I know I probably would’ve enjoyed it before.\n\nEven the rose beds outside… I used to really care about them. I’d notice every new bud, fuss over them, prune things, check the soil. Now I look at them and think, “I should really get out there,” but it feels like work, so I keep delaying it. It makes me sad, honestly, because those were things that felt like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping differently than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my sleep has definitely been different. There are nights where I’ll only sleep maybe three or four hours, and then I wake up like I’ve had a full night, almost too awake. It’s not that groggy, dragging-myself-out-of-bed feeling. I’ll wake up and feel charged, like my brain is already going before the sun is even up.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, “Oh, I should write that blog post,” or I’ll start planning some project in my head, like making lists or figuring out steps, and it feels really urgent and doable in the moment. It’s strange because I know three or four hours doesn’t sound like enough sleep, and usually I’d expect to feel awful, but on those mornings I don’t. I feel ready to get up and do things, sometimes before anyone else is even awake.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you only sleep a few hours and feel very energized, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it’s not just “I feel a little more optimistic.” It can get bigger than that. During those times, I’ll suddenly feel very sure of myself, like I can see this whole path in front of me and everyone else just hasn’t caught up yet.\n\nOne thing that happens is with this art-therapy workshop idea I’ve had. I haven’t even finished the training yet, so I know, sitting here saying it, that maybe I should be more measured about it. But when I’m in that energized state, I become completely convinced that the workshop is going to change people’s lives in this really major way. Like not just be helpful, but really important. I’ll think, “This is what I’m meant to do, this could reach so many people, this could transform how they heal,” and it feels absolutely true in the moment.\n\nAnd I can get kind of carried away with that feeling of capability, like I’m already almost there even though there are still steps I haven’t done. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because part of me knows I’m still learning, but at the time I feel unusually certain that I can make it happen and that it’s going to be much bigger than maybe it realistically is.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean… I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself talking more in a way that stands out. I’m already someone who can explain things with a lot of detail, as you can probably tell, so it’s a little hard for me to separate “me being me” from something unusual.\n\nDuring those energized times, I might have more thoughts I want to get out, but I can’t say for sure that I’m talking nonstop or that other people have pointed it out to me. I haven’t really been tracking that part. It’s more that my mind feels busy, but whether that turns into me talking more than usual… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it or maybe ask someone who’s around me.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you start taking on more projects or doing more activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the clearer things I notice, once I look back on it. In the moment it just feels like I’m being productive, like I’m finally getting my life organized, but then if I step back it’s a lot all at once.\n\nAt my busiest, I’ll do things like sign up for committees or volunteer for something before I’ve really thought through whether I have the time or energy for it. And then the same day I might be messaging people about workshop ideas, like sending these long messages about art-therapy-type things I want to put together, trying to connect with people or get feedback or make plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll also start several blog drafts, not just one. I’ll have one idea and then another one, and I’ll think, “Oh, this needs to be written too,” and suddenly I have multiple unfinished drafts open or notes everywhere. And then on top of that I’ll start making these big plans for the garden, like what needs to be planted, how it should be laid out, what supplies I need, what I could do differently this year… all in the same day sometimes.\n\nSo yes, I do take on more. It doesn’t always look dramatic from the outside, maybe, but for me it’s like I suddenly have ten tracks running at once and they all feel important and possible right then.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you become more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like my body gets restless in a way that feels different from normal motivation. I don’t just think about doing things — I’m up and moving before I’ve really decided to be.\n\nI’ll start cleaning shelves, like pulling everything off and wiping them down, and then that turns into rearranging my art supplies because suddenly the whole setup feels wrong or inefficient. Then I’ll find a notebook and start drafting poems, or little pieces of writing, and I’ll be moving between that and something else like I can’t stay in one place long enough.\n\nAnd I pace a lot, especially through the garden. I’ll go out there and walk around looking at the plants or the beds, thinking about what should be moved or fixed, and it’s not exactly relaxing. It’s more like… if I stop, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Like there’s this pressure inside me to keep doing something, anything, even if it’s not all connected.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you feel unusually cheerful or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d call it “euphoric” exactly, because that word sounds so extreme, but there are definitely times when I feel unusually bright, like everything is suddenly possible and I can handle anything. It’s almost like I’m lighter, or like I’ve finally clicked into the version of myself I’m supposed to be. I’ll feel very confident, very energized, like, “Okay, now I can fix all of this, now I can do everything.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t always stay cheerful. It can flip really fast. I can go from feeling unstoppable to suddenly furious over something that probably looks small from the outside. Like if my husband moves my paints and I can’t find the exact color or brush I wanted, I’ll get so angry, like he’s ruined the whole flow of what I was doing. Or if he asks about a receipt, just a normal question, I can feel this heat come up in me like he’s accusing me or trying to control me, even if I know later that maybe he wasn’t.\n\nSo yes, there’s that bright, almost overly positive feeling, but it’s mixed with this irritability that can come on really sharply. It’s not just happy. It’s more like everything is turned up too high.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, have they caused problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… they have. I mean, it’s not like everything completely falls apart, but it does create problems, and sometimes I only realize it after I’ve already made things more complicated.\n\nOne big thing is that I’ve fallen behind on my facilitator training. I’ll have all these ideas and intentions, and I’ll start explaining things or planning things in this very expanded way, but then it gets hard to organize it into the actual steps I’m supposed to be doing. So instead of moving forward, I end up with pages of notes or half-plans, and then I’m behind.\n\nThe same thing happens with my private blog work. I’ll have a lot I want to say, but the drafts get scattered, or my explanations get too long and tangled, and then I can’t finish them properly. It’s frustrating because in the moment I feel like I’m doing so much, but the finished work doesn’t match that feeling.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it causes tension with my husband. When I’m in that state, normal routines become harder to keep. Little things turn into discussions or arguments, and then the house rhythm gets thrown off. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

{"candidates":["F31.8","F32.9","F50.8"],"note":"History now supports a bipolar spectrum disorder rather than isolated depression: decreased need for sleep, elevated/irritable mood, increased goal-directed activity, overconfidence/grandiosity, and functional consequences suggest hypomanic episodes. This narrows bipolar from unspecified (F31.9) to other bipolar affective disorder (F31.8). Eating symptoms still fit an other specified eating disorder pattern; anorexia/bulimia features remain unsupported.","is_final":false}

I’m already someone who can explain things with a lot of detail, as you can probably tell, so it’s a little hard for me to separate “me being me” from something unusual.\n\nDuring those energized times, I might have more thoughts I want to get out, but I can’t say for sure that I’m talking nonstop or that other people have pointed it out to me. I haven’t really been tracking that part. It’s more that my mind feels busy, but whether that turns into me talking more than usual… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it or maybe ask someone who’s around me.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you start taking on more projects or doing more activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the clearer things I notice, once I look back on it. In the moment it just feels like I’m being productive, like I’m finally getting my life organized, but then if I step back it’s a lot all at once.\n\nAt my busiest, I’ll do things like sign up for committees or volunteer for something before I’ve really thought through whether I have the time or energy for it. And then the same day I might be messaging people about workshop ideas, like sending these long messages about art-therapy-type things I want to put together, trying to connect with people or get feedback or make plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll also start several blog drafts, not just one. I’ll have one idea and then another one, and I’ll think, “Oh, this needs to be written too,” and suddenly I have multiple unfinished drafts open or notes everywhere. And then on top of that I’ll start making these big plans for the garden, like what needs to be planted, how it should be laid out, what supplies I need, what I could do differently this year… all in the same day sometimes.\n\nSo yes, I do take on more. It doesn’t always look dramatic from the outside, maybe, but for me it’s like I suddenly have ten tracks running at once and they all feel important and possible right then.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you become more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like my body gets restless in a way that feels different from normal motivation. I don’t just think about doing things — I’m up and moving before I’ve really decided to be.\n\nI’ll start cleaning shelves, like pulling everything off and wiping them down, and then that turns into rearranging my art supplies because suddenly the whole setup feels wrong or inefficient. Then I’ll find a notebook and start drafting poems, or little pieces of writing, and I’ll be moving between that and something else like I can’t stay in one place long enough.\n\nAnd I pace a lot, especially through the garden. I’ll go out there and walk around looking at the plants or the beds, thinking about what should be moved or fixed, and it’s not exactly relaxing. It’s more like… if I stop, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Like there’s this pressure inside me to keep doing something, anything, even if it’s not all connected.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you feel unusually cheerful or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d call it “euphoric” exactly, because that word sounds so extreme, but there are definitely times when I feel unusually bright, like everything is suddenly possible and I can handle anything. It’s almost like I’m lighter, or like I’ve finally clicked into the version of myself I’m supposed to be. I’ll feel very confident, very energized, like, “Okay, now I can fix all of this, now I can do everything.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t always stay cheerful. It can flip really fast. I can go from feeling unstoppable to suddenly furious over something that probably looks small from the outside. Like if my husband moves my paints and I can’t find the exact color or brush I wanted, I’ll get so angry, like he’s ruined the whole flow of what I was doing. Or if he asks about a receipt, just a normal question, I can feel this heat come up in me like he’s accusing me or trying to control me, even if I know later that maybe he wasn’t.\n\nSo yes, there’s that bright, almost overly positive feeling, but it’s mixed with this irritability that can come on really sharply. It’s not just happy. It’s more like everything is turned up too high.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, have they caused problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… they have. I mean, it’s not like everything completely falls apart, but it does create problems, and sometimes I only realize it after I’ve already made things more complicated.\n\nOne big thing is that I’ve fallen behind on my facilitator training. I’ll have all these ideas and intentions, and I’ll start explaining things or planning things in this very expanded way, but then it gets hard to organize it into the actual steps I’m supposed to be doing. So instead of moving forward, I end up with pages of notes or half-plans, and then I’m behind.\n\nThe same thing happens with my private blog work. I’ll have a lot I want to say, but the drafts get scattered, or my explanations get too long and tangled, and then I can’t finish them properly. It’s frustrating because in the moment I feel like I’m doing so much, but the finished work doesn’t match that feeling.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it causes tension with my husband. When I’m in that state, normal routines become harder to keep. Little things turn into discussions or arguments, and then the house rhythm gets thrown off. So it’s mild maybe compared to what it could be, but it definitely affects my training, my writing, and how steady things feel at home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, not like intentionally making myself throw up or anything. Afterward I usually feel gross and kind of ashamed, and sometimes my stomach feels too full, like I overdid it, but I don’t go to the bathroom to make myself vomit. I mostly just sit with that awful feeling and tell myself I won’t do it again, and then sometimes it happens again anyway.\n\nDoctor: Are you very afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “very afraid,” exactly. I mean, I don’t love the idea of gaining weight—who does, really—but it’s not like I’m constantly weighing myself or panicking over every bite because I think I’ll get fat.\n\nIt’s more that after I eat a lot, I feel uncomfortable and kind of upset with myself, and then I start thinking, “Why did I do that?” But I’m not sure it’s specifically about weight gain. It’s more like feeling out of control, or like I did something I shouldn’t have. So… no, I don’t think I’m very afraid of gaining weight, not in a way that’s driving everything.\n\nDoctor: When those late-night eating episodes happen, do you feel unable to stop eating once you’ve started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it does feel that way, but it’s hard to explain because it’s not always like I’m starving or anything. It’s more like once I’m standing there in the kitchen, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll have a few crackers or something sweet, and then it kind of turns into another thing, and then another thing. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough,” but I don’t really walk away right away.\n\nAnd that’s what feels so weird compared to the weeks where I barely touch dinner. I’ll sit down with food and just pick at it, like I can’t be bothered or it doesn’t appeal to me, and then later there are these nights where I’m eating in this restless way. I don’t know if it’s that I physically can’t stop, but it feels like I don’t stop when I mean to. Then afterward I feel embarrassed, like, why was I doing that alone in the kitchen?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have been. Not every single minute, and it’s not always dramatic, but I get these stretches where everything in the house just feels kind of gray and heavy. Like I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and nothing terrible has even happened that day, but I feel empty, like the air got sucked out of me or something.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside I probably look like I’m just sitting there, maybe drinking coffee or staring at the counter, but inside it feels… flat. Heavy. Like there’s this sadness settled over everything, even ordinary things. Sometimes I’ll think, “What is wrong with me? Nothing happened today,” and that almost makes it worse because I can’t point to a reason. It’s just there.\n\nDoctor: When those late-night episodes happen, do you eat what most people would consider a large amount of food for that period of time?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know how “large” it would look to someone else, exactly. It’s not like I’m sitting down with a whole prepared meal or anything, but it can add up because I’ll be standing there in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep, and it starts with crackers, then maybe something sweet, then I go back for more crackers, and then I’m opening another cabinet.\n\nSo in that period of time, yeah, sometimes it feels like more than I meant to eat, especially because it’s late and I already had the chance to eat dinner earlier. But it’s also weird because some weeks I barely touch dinner at all, like I’ll just pick at it and not really want it, and then other nights I’m eating all this snack food in the kitchen. That back-and-forth is really what pushed me to come in, honestly—my eating feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you barely touch dinner, do you feel like you’ve lost your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… during those weeks, it does feel like my appetite just kind of drops off. I’ll make dinner or sit down with everyone, and then when the plate is in front of me, I just don’t really want it. I’ll take a few bites, move things around, maybe tell myself I should eat more, but it’s like my body isn’t interested.\n\nAnd that’s part of what’s been so confusing, because it’s not consistent. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like food just doesn’t appeal to me much, and then other nights I’m up late after everyone’s gone to bed, standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. That back-and-forth is really what made me feel like something was off enough to come talk about it. My eating just feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I never enjoy anything at all, but things I used to actually look forward to feel weirdly hard to start now. Like my book club—I used to pick out the book and think about what I wanted to say, and now even reading the chapters feels like this assignment I’m behind on. I’ll see the book sitting there and just feel tired before I even open it.\n\nAnd cooking used to be something I could get absorbed in, like planning a meal or trying a new recipe, but lately it feels more like another chore in the line of chores. Same with going to galleries. I used to love wandering around and looking at everything slowly, and now I keep putting it off, even when I know I probably would’ve enjoyed it before.\n\nEven the rose beds outside… I used to really care about them. I’d notice every new bud, fuss over them, prune things, check the soil. Now I look at them and think, “I should really get out there,” but it feels like work, so I keep delaying it. It makes me sad, honestly, because those were things that felt like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping differently than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my sleep has definitely been different. There are nights where I’ll only sleep maybe three or four hours, and then I wake up like I’ve had a full night, almost too awake. It’s not that groggy, dragging-myself-out-of-bed feeling. I’ll wake up and feel charged, like my brain is already going before the sun is even up.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, “Oh, I should write that blog post,” or I’ll start planning some project in my head, like making lists or figuring out steps, and it feels really urgent and doable in the moment. It’s strange because I know three or four hours doesn’t sound like enough sleep, and usually I’d expect to feel awful, but on those mornings I don’t. I feel ready to get up and do things, sometimes before anyone else is even awake.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you only sleep a few hours and feel very energized, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it’s not just “I feel a little more optimistic.” It can get bigger than that. During those times, I’ll suddenly feel very sure of myself, like I can see this whole path in front of me and everyone else just hasn’t caught up yet.\n\nOne thing that happens is with this art-therapy workshop idea I’ve had. I haven’t even finished the training yet, so I know, sitting here saying it, that maybe I should be more measured about it. But when I’m in that energized state, I become completely convinced that the workshop is going to change people’s lives in this really major way. Like not just be helpful, but really important. I’ll think, “This is what I’m meant to do, this could reach so many people, this could transform how they heal,” and it feels absolutely true in the moment.\n\nAnd I can get kind of carried away with that feeling of capability, like I’m already almost there even though there are still steps I haven’t done. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because part of me knows I’m still learning, but at the time I feel unusually certain that I can make it happen and that it’s going to be much bigger than maybe it realistically is.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean… I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself talking more in a way that stands out. I’m already someone who can explain things with a lot of detail, as you can probably tell, so it’s a little hard for me to separate “me being me” from something unusual.\n\nDuring those energized times, I might have more thoughts I want to get out, but I can’t say for sure that I’m talking nonstop or that other people have pointed it out to me. I haven’t really been tracking that part. It’s more that my mind feels busy, but whether that turns into me talking more than usual… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it or maybe ask someone who’s around me.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you start taking on more projects or doing more activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the clearer things I notice, once I look back on it. In the moment it just feels like I’m being productive, like I’m finally getting my life organized, but then if I step back it’s a lot all at once.\n\nAt my busiest, I’ll do things like sign up for committees or volunteer for something before I’ve really thought through whether I have the time or energy for it. And then the same day I might be messaging people about workshop ideas, like sending these long messages about art-therapy-type things I want to put together, trying to connect with people or get feedback or make plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll also start several blog drafts, not just one. I’ll have one idea and then another one, and I’ll think, “Oh, this needs to be written too,” and suddenly I have multiple unfinished drafts open or notes everywhere. And then on top of that I’ll start making these big plans for the garden, like what needs to be planted, how it should be laid out, what supplies I need, what I could do differently this year… all in the same day sometimes.\n\nSo yes, I do take on more. It doesn’t always look dramatic from the outside, maybe, but for me it’s like I suddenly have ten tracks running at once and they all feel important and possible right then.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you become more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like my body gets restless in a way that feels different from normal motivation. I don’t just think about doing things — I’m up and moving before I’ve really decided to be.\n\nI’ll start cleaning shelves, like pulling everything off and wiping them down, and then that turns into rearranging my art supplies because suddenly the whole setup feels wrong or inefficient. Then I’ll find a notebook and start drafting poems, or little pieces of writing, and I’ll be moving between that and something else like I can’t stay in one place long enough.\n\nAnd I pace a lot, especially through the garden. I’ll go out there and walk around looking at the plants or the beds, thinking about what should be moved or fixed, and it’s not exactly relaxing. It’s more like… if I stop, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Like there’s this pressure inside me to keep doing something, anything, even if it’s not all connected.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you feel unusually cheerful or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d call it “euphoric” exactly, because that word sounds so extreme, but there are definitely times when I feel unusually bright, like everything is suddenly possible and I can handle anything. It’s almost like I’m lighter, or like I’ve finally clicked into the version of myself I’m supposed to be. I’ll feel very confident, very energized, like, “Okay, now I can fix all of this, now I can do everything.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t always stay cheerful. It can flip really fast. I can go from feeling unstoppable to suddenly furious over something that probably looks small from the outside. Like if my husband moves my paints and I can’t find the exact color or brush I wanted, I’ll get so angry, like he’s ruined the whole flow of what I was doing. Or if he asks about a receipt, just a normal question, I can feel this heat come up in me like he’s accusing me or trying to control me, even if I know later that maybe he wasn’t.\n\nSo yes, there’s that bright, almost overly positive feeling, but it’s mixed with this irritability that can come on really sharply. It’s not just happy. It’s more like everything is turned up too high.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, have they caused problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… they have. I mean, it’s not like everything completely falls apart, but it does create problems, and sometimes I only realize it after I’ve already made things more complicated.\n\nOne big thing is that I’ve fallen behind on my facilitator training. I’ll have all these ideas and intentions, and I’ll start explaining things or planning things in this very expanded way, but then it gets hard to organize it into the actual steps I’m supposed to be doing. So instead of moving forward, I end up with pages of notes or half-plans, and then I’m behind.\n\nThe same thing happens with my private blog work. I’ll have a lot I want to say, but the drafts get scattered, or my explanations get too long and tangled, and then I can’t finish them properly. It’s frustrating because in the moment I feel like I’m doing so much, but the finished work doesn’t match that feeling.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it causes tension with my husband. When I’m in that state, normal routines become harder to keep. Little things turn into discussions or arguments, and then the house rhythm gets thrown off. So it’s mild maybe compared to what it could be, but it definitely affects my training, my writing, and how steady things feel at home.\n\nDoctor: How long do these energized periods usually last?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for… I think about a year and eight months now, if I’m counting it right. Not every single day that whole time, of course, but these energized stretches — where I get overcommitted and wound up and take on too much — they’ve been part of my life for roughly that long.\n\nI didn’t recognize it as a pattern right away. At first I thought I was just having productive phases, or trying to get myself back on track, but looking back, it’s been about a year and eight months that this has been happening in a noticeable way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I can go from feeling unstoppable to suddenly furious over something that probably looks small from the outside. Like if my husband moves my paints and I can’t find the exact color or brush I wanted, I’ll get so angry, like he’s ruined the whole flow of what I was doing. Or if he asks about a receipt, just a normal question, I can feel this heat come up in me like he’s accusing me or trying to control me, even if I know later that maybe he wasn’t.\n\nSo yes, there’s that bright, almost overly positive feeling, but it’s mixed with this irritability that can come on really sharply. It’s not just happy. It’s more like everything is turned up too high.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, have they caused problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… they have. I mean, it’s not like everything completely falls apart, but it does create problems, and sometimes I only realize it after I’ve already made things more complicated.\n\nOne big thing is that I’ve fallen behind on my facilitator training. I’ll have all these ideas and intentions, and I’ll start explaining things or planning things in this very expanded way, but then it gets hard to organize it into the actual steps I’m supposed to be doing. So instead of moving forward, I end up with pages of notes or half-plans, and then I’m behind.\n\nThe same thing happens with my private blog work. I’ll have a lot I want to say, but the drafts get scattered, or my explanations get too long and tangled, and then I can’t finish them properly. It’s frustrating because in the moment I feel like I’m doing so much, but the finished work doesn’t match that feeling.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it causes tension with my husband. When I’m in that state, normal routines become harder to keep. Little things turn into discussions or arguments, and then the house rhythm gets thrown off. So it’s mild maybe compared to what it could be, but it definitely affects my training, my writing, and how steady things feel at home.\n\nDoctor: How long do these energized periods usually last?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for… I think about a year and eight months now, if I’m counting it right. Not every single day that whole time, of course, but these energized stretches — where I get overcommitted and wound up and take on too much — they’ve been part of my life for roughly that long.\n\nI didn’t recognize it as a pattern right away. At first I thought I was just having productive phases, or trying to get myself back on track, but looking back, it’s been about a year and eight months that this has been happening in a noticeable way.\n\nDoctor: When one of those energized periods begins, does it usually last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to measure it as exactly four days, because I don’t always mark the start clearly when it’s happening. It’s more like I realize afterward, “Oh, I’ve been in that wound-up, overcommitted mode again.”\n\nWhat I can say is that these stretches have been part of my life for about a year and eight months now. They come and go during that time, but it’s been a noticeable pattern for that long. Sometimes it feels like it lasts long enough that it’s not just one odd day, but I’d have to think carefully to say whether every episode is at least four days in a row.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. At first I thought I was just having productive phases, or trying to get myself back on track, but looking back, it’s been about a year and eight months that this has been happening in a noticeable way.\n\nDoctor: When one of those energized periods begins, does it usually last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to measure it as exactly four days, because I don’t always mark the start clearly when it’s happening. They come and go during that time, but it’s been a noticeable pattern for that long. Sometimes it feels like it lasts long enough that it’s not just one odd day, but I’d have to think carefully to say whether every episode is at least four days in a row.\n\n[Last Question]\nThinking of one typical energized period, has it ever lasted at least 4 days in a row?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I think… yes, it has felt like it lasts more than just a day or two, but I get a little tangled when I try to pin it down exactly to “four days,” because I don’t always notice the beginning in real time.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just been… really all over the place lately. I’ll make dinner or sit down with everyone, and then when the plate is in front of me, I just don’t really want it. I’ll take a few bites, move things around, maybe tell myself I should eat more, but it’s like my body isn’t interested.\n\nAnd that’s part of what’s been so confusing, because it’s not consistent. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like food just doesn’t appeal to me much, and then other nights I’m up late after everyone’s gone to bed, standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. That back-and-forth is really what made me feel like something was off enough to come talk about it. My eating just feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I never enjoy anything at all, but things I used to actually look forward to feel weirdly hard to start now. Like my book club—I used to pick out the book and think about what I wanted to say, and now even reading the chapters feels like this assignment I’m behind on. I’ll see the book sitting there and just feel tired before I even open it.\n\nAnd cooking used to be something I could get absorbed in, like planning a meal or trying a new recipe, but lately it feels more like another chore in the line of chores. I used to love wandering around and looking at everything slowly, and now I keep putting it off, even when I know I probably would’ve enjoyed it before.\n\nEven the rose beds outside… I used to really care about them. I’ll wake up and feel charged, like my brain is already going before the sun is even up.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, “Oh, I should write that blog post,” or I’ll start planning some project in my head, like making lists or figuring out steps, and it feels really urgent and doable in the moment. I feel ready to get up and do things, sometimes before anyone else is even awake.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you only sleep a few hours and feel very energized, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll think, “This is what I’m meant to do, this could reach so many people, this could transform how they heal,” and it feels absolutely true in the moment.\n\nAnd I can get kind of carried away with that feeling of capability, like I’m already almost there even though there are still steps I haven’t done. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because part of me knows I’m still learning, but at the time I feel unusually certain that I can make it happen and that it’s going to be much bigger than maybe it realistically is.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking that part. It’s more that my mind feels busy, but whether that turns into me talking more than usual… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it or maybe ask someone who’s around me.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you start taking on more projects or doing more activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s more like… if I stop, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Like there’s this pressure inside me to keep doing something, anything, even if it’s not all connected.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you feel unusually cheerful or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d call it “euphoric” exactly, because that word sounds so extreme, but there are definitely times when I feel unusually bright, like everything is suddenly possible and I can handle anything. It’s almost like I’m lighter, or like I’ve finally clicked into the version of myself I’m supposed to be. I’ll feel very confident, very energized, like, “Okay, now I can fix all of this, now I can do everything.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t always stay cheerful. It can flip really fast. I can go from feeling unstoppable to suddenly furious over something that probably looks small from the outside. Like I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and nothing terrible has even happened that day, but I feel empty, like the air got sucked out of me or something.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside I probably look like I’m just sitting there, maybe drinking coffee or staring at the counter, but inside it feels… flat. Heavy. Like there’s this sadness settled over everything, even ordinary things. Sometimes I’ll think, “What is wrong with me? Nothing happened today,” and that almost makes it worse because I can’t point to a reason. It’s just there.\n\nDoctor: When those late-night episodes happen, do you eat what most people would consider a large amount of food for that period of time?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know how “large” it would look to someone else, exactly. It’s not like I’m sitting down with a whole prepared meal or anything, but it can add up because I’ll be standing there in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep, and it starts with crackers, then maybe something sweet, then I go back for more crackers, and then I’m opening another cabinet.\n\nSo in that period of time, yeah, sometimes it feels like more than I meant to eat, especially because it’s late and I already had the chance to eat dinner earlier. But it’s also weird because some weeks I barely touch dinner at all, like I’ll just pick at it and not really want it, and then other nights I’m eating all this snack food in the kitchen. That back-and-forth is really what pushed me to come in, honestly—my eating feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you barely touch dinner, do you feel like you’ve lost your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… during those weeks, it does feel like my appetite just kind of drops off. I’ll make dinner or sit down with everyone, and then when the plate is in front of me, I just don’t really want it. I’ll take a few bites, move things around, maybe tell myself I should eat more, but it’s like my body isn’t interested.\n\nAnd that’s part of what’s been so confusing, because it’s not consistent. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like food just doesn’t appeal to me much, and then other nights I’m up late after everyone’s gone to bed, standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. That back-and-forth is really what made me feel like something was off enough to come talk about it. My eating just feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I never enjoy anything at all, but things I used to actually look forward to feel weirdly hard to start now. Like my book club—I used to pick out the book and think about what I wanted to say, and now even reading the chapters feels like this assignment I’m behind on. I’ll see the book sitting there and just feel tired before I even open it.\n\nAnd cooking used to be something I could get absorbed in, like planning a meal or trying a new recipe, but lately it feels more like another chore in the line of chores. Same with going to galleries. I used to love wandering around and looking at everything slowly, and now I keep putting it off, even when I know I probably would’ve enjoyed it before.\n\nEven the rose beds outside… I used to really care about them. I’d notice every new bud, fuss over them, prune things, check the soil. Now I look at them and think, “I should really get out there,” but it feels like work, so I keep delaying it. It makes me sad, honestly, because those were things that felt like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping differently than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my sleep has definitely been different. There are nights where I’ll only sleep maybe three or four hours, and then I wake up like I’ve had a full night, almost too awake. It’s not that groggy, dragging-myself-out-of-bed feeling. I’ll wake up and feel charged, like my brain is already going before the sun is even up.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, “Oh, I should write that blog post,” or I’ll start planning some project in my head, like making lists or figuring out steps, and it feels really urgent and doable in the moment. It’s strange because I know three or four hours doesn’t sound like enough sleep, and usually I’d expect to feel awful, but on those mornings I don’t. I feel ready to get up and do things, sometimes before anyone else is even awake.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you only sleep a few hours and feel very energized, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it’s not just “I feel a little more optimistic.” It can get bigger than that. During those times, I’ll suddenly feel very sure of myself, like I can see this whole path in front of me and everyone else just hasn’t caught up yet.\n\nOne thing that happens is with this art-therapy workshop idea I’ve had. I haven’t even finished the training yet, so I know, sitting here saying it, that maybe I should be more measured about it. But when I’m in that energized state, I become completely convinced that the workshop is going to change people’s lives in this really major way. Like not just be helpful, but really important. I’ll think, “This is what I’m meant to do, this could reach so many people, this could transform how they heal,” and it feels absolutely true in the moment.\n\nAnd I can get kind of carried away with that feeling of capability, like I’m already almost there even though there are still steps I haven’t done. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because part of me knows I’m still learning, but at the time I feel unusually certain that I can make it happen and that it’s going to be much bigger than maybe it realistically is.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean… I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself talking more in a way that stands out. I’m already someone who can explain things with a lot of detail, as you can probably tell, so it’s a little hard for me to separate “me being me” from something unusual.\n\nDuring those energized times, I might have more thoughts I want to get out, but I can’t say for sure that I’m talking nonstop or that other people have pointed it out to me. I haven’t really been tracking that part. It’s more that my mind feels busy, but whether that turns into me talking more than usual… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it or maybe ask someone who’s around me.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you start taking on more projects or doing more activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the clearer things I notice, once I look back on it. In the moment it just feels like I’m being productive, like I’m finally getting my life organized, but then if I step back it’s a lot all at once.\n\nAt my busiest, I’ll do things like sign up for committees or volunteer for something before I’ve really thought through whether I have the time or energy for it. And then the same day I might be messaging people about workshop ideas, like sending these long messages about art-therapy-type things I want to put together, trying to connect with people or get feedback or make plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll also start several blog drafts, not just one. I’ll have one idea and then another one, and I’ll think, “Oh, this needs to be written too,” and suddenly I have multiple unfinished drafts open or notes everywhere. And then on top of that I’ll start making these big plans for the garden, like what needs to be planted, how it should be laid out, what supplies I need, what I could do differently this year… all in the same day sometimes.\n\nSo yes, I do take on more. It doesn’t always look dramatic from the outside, maybe, but for me it’s like I suddenly have ten tracks running at once and they all feel important and possible right then.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you become more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like my body gets restless in a way that feels different from normal motivation. I don’t just think about doing things — I’m up and moving before I’ve really decided to be.\n\nI’ll start cleaning shelves, like pulling everything off and wiping them down, and then that turns into rearranging my art supplies because suddenly the whole setup feels wrong or inefficient. Then I’ll find a notebook and start drafting poems, or little pieces of writing, and I’ll be moving between that and something else like I can’t stay in one place long enough.\n\nAnd I pace a lot, especially through the garden. I’ll go out there and walk around looking at the plants or the beds, thinking about what should be moved or fixed, and it’s not exactly relaxing. It’s more like… if I stop, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Like there’s this pressure inside me to keep doing something, anything, even if it’s not all connected.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you feel unusually cheerful or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d call it “euphoric” exactly, because that word sounds so extreme, but there are definitely times when I feel unusually bright, like everything is suddenly possible and I can handle anything. It’s almost like I’m lighter, or like I’ve finally clicked into the version of myself I’m supposed to be. I’ll feel very confident, very energized, like, “Okay, now I can fix all of this, now I can do everything.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t always stay cheerful. It can flip really fast. I can go from feeling unstoppable to suddenly furious over something that probably looks small from the outside. Like if my husband moves my paints and I can’t find the exact color or brush I wanted, I’ll get so angry, like he’s ruined the whole flow of what I was doing. Or if he asks about a receipt, just a normal question, I can feel this heat come up in me like he’s accusing me or trying to control me, even if I know later that maybe he wasn’t.\n\nSo yes, there’s that bright, almost overly positive feeling, but it’s mixed with this irritability that can come on really sharply. It’s not just happy. It’s more like everything is turned up too high.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, have they caused problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… they have. I mean, it’s not like everything completely falls apart, but it does create problems, and sometimes I only realize it after I’ve already made things more complicated.\n\nOne big thing is that I’ve fallen behind on my facilitator training. I’ll have all these ideas and intentions, and I’ll start explaining things or planning things in this very expanded way, but then it gets hard to organize it into the actual steps I’m supposed to be doing. So instead of moving forward, I end up with pages of notes or half-plans, and then I’m behind.\n\nThe same thing happens with my private blog work. I’ll have a lot I want to say, but the drafts get scattered, or my explanations get too long and tangled, and then I can’t finish them properly. It’s frustrating because in the moment I feel like I’m doing so much, but the finished work doesn’t match that feeling.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it causes tension with my husband. When I’m in that state, normal routines become harder to keep. Little things turn into discussions or arguments, and then the house rhythm gets thrown off. So it’s mild maybe compared to what it could be, but it definitely affects my training, my writing, and how steady things feel at home.\n\nDoctor: How long do these energized periods usually last?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for… I think about a year and eight months now, if I’m counting it right. Not every single day that whole time, of course, but these energized stretches — where I get overcommitted and wound up and take on too much — they’ve been part of my life for roughly that long.\n\nI didn’t recognize it as a pattern right away. At first I thought I was just having productive phases, or trying to get myself back on track, but looking back, it’s been about a year and eight months that this has been happening in a noticeable way.\n\nDoctor: When one of those energized periods begins, does it usually last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to measure it as exactly four days, because I don’t always mark the start clearly when it’s happening. It’s more like I realize afterward, “Oh, I’ve been in that wound-up, overcommitted mode again.”\n\nWhat I can say is that these stretches have been part of my life for about a year and eight months now. They come and go during that time, but it’s been a noticeable pattern for that long. Sometimes it feels like it lasts long enough that it’s not just one odd day, but I’d have to think carefully to say whether every episode is at least four days in a row.\n\nDoctor: Thinking of one typical energized period, has it ever lasted at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it has felt like it lasts more than just a day or two, but I get a little tangled when I try to pin it down exactly to “four days,” because I don’t always notice the beginning in real time.\n\nWhat I can say is that these energized, overcommitted stretches have been happening as a pattern for about a year and eight months. They come in waves, and when I’m in one, it doesn’t feel like a brief mood for an afternoon — it feels like I’m sort of living in that mode for a stretch. But if you’re asking me to identify one typical one and count the days, I’d say it probably has lasted at least four days at times, even though I don’t always track it neatly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, I don’t think I’ve ever been hospitalized specifically because of one of those energized periods. I mean, they can get messy in the sense that I take on too much and feel kind of wound up, and afterward I can look back and think, “Why did I do all that?” But I haven’t had a time where I was admitted to a hospital because of it.

At least not the way I understand what you’re asking — like needing inpatient care because that energized state got out of control. That hasn’t happened.

That back-and-forth is really what made me feel like something was off enough to come talk about it. My eating just feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I never enjoy anything at all, but things I used to actually look forward to feel weirdly hard to start now. They come in waves, and when I’m in one, it doesn’t feel like a brief mood for an afternoon — it feels like I’m sort of living in that mode for a stretch. Then I’ll find a notebook and start drafting poems, or little pieces of writing, and I’ll be moving between that and something else like I can’t stay in one place long enough.\n\nAnd I pace a lot, especially through the garden. I’ll go out there and walk around looking at the plants or the beds, thinking about what should be moved or fixed, and it’s not exactly relaxing. It’s more like… if I stop, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Like there’s this pressure inside me to keep doing something, anything, even if it’s not all connected.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you feel unusually cheerful or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d call it “euphoric” exactly, because that word sounds so extreme, but there are definitely times when I feel unusually bright, like everything is suddenly possible and I can handle anything. It’s almost like I’m lighter, or like I’ve finally clicked into the version of myself I’m supposed to be. I’ll feel very confident, very energized, like, “Okay, now I can fix all of this, now I can do everything.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t always stay cheerful. It can flip really fast. I can go from feeling unstoppable to suddenly furious over something that probably looks small from the outside. Like if my husband moves my paints and I can’t find the exact color or brush I wanted, I’ll get so angry, like he’s ruined the whole flow of what I was doing. Or if he asks about a receipt, just a normal question, I can feel this heat come up in me like he’s accusing me or trying to control me, even if I know later that maybe he wasn’t.\n\nSo yes, there’s that bright, almost overly positive feeling, but it’s mixed with this irritability that can come on really sharply. It’s not just happy. It’s more like everything is turned up too high.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, have they caused problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… they have. I mean, it’s not like everything completely falls apart, but it does create problems, and sometimes I only realize it after I’ve already made things more complicated.\n\nOne big thing is that I’ve fallen behind on my facilitator training. I’ll have all these ideas and intentions, and I’ll start explaining things or planning things in this very expanded way, but then it gets hard to organize it into the actual steps I’m supposed to be doing. So instead of moving forward, I end up with pages of notes or half-plans, and then I’m behind.\n\nThe same thing happens with my private blog work. I’ll have a lot I want to say, but the drafts get scattered, or my explanations get too long and tangled, and then I can’t finish them properly. It’s frustrating because in the moment I feel like I’m doing so much, but the finished work doesn’t match that feeling.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it causes tension with my husband. When I’m in that state, normal routines become harder to keep. Little things turn into discussions or arguments, and then the house rhythm gets thrown off. So it’s mild maybe compared to what it could be, but it definitely affects my training, my writing, and how steady things feel at home.\n\nDoctor: How long do these energized periods usually last?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for… I think about a year and eight months now, if I’m counting it right. Not every single day that whole time, of course, but these energized stretches — where I get overcommitted and wound up and take on too much — they’ve been part of my life for roughly that long.\n\nI didn’t recognize it as a pattern right away. At first I thought I was just having productive phases, or trying to get myself back on track, but looking back, it’s been about a year and eight months that this has been happening in a noticeable way.\n\nDoctor: When one of those energized periods begins, does it usually last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to measure it as exactly four days, because I don’t always mark the start clearly when it’s happening. It’s more like I realize afterward, “Oh, I’ve been in that wound-up, overcommitted mode again.”\n\nWhat I can say is that these stretches have been part of my life for about a year and eight months now. They come and go during that time, but it’s been a noticeable pattern for that long. Sometimes it feels like it lasts long enough that it’s not just one odd day, but I’d have to think carefully to say whether every episode is at least four days in a row.\n\nDoctor: Thinking of one typical energized period, has it ever lasted at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it has felt like it lasts more than just a day or two, but I get a little tangled when I try to pin it down exactly to “four days,” because I don’t always notice the beginning in real time.\n\nWhat I can say is that these energized, overcommitted stretches have been happening as a pattern for about a year and eight months. They come in waves, and when I’m in one, it doesn’t feel like a brief mood for an afternoon — it feels like I’m sort of living in that mode for a stretch. But if you’re asking me to identify one typical one and count the days, I’d say it probably has lasted at least four days at times, even though I don’t always track it neatly.\n\nDoctor: During one of these energized periods, have you ever needed to be hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever been hospitalized specifically because of one of those energized periods. I mean, they can get messy in the sense that I take on too much and feel kind of wound up, and afterward I can look back and think, “Why did I do all that?” But I haven’t had a time where I was admitted to a hospital because of it.\n\nAt least not the way I understand what you’re asking — like needing inpatient care because that energized state got out of control. Yeah… those gray periods, the heavy ones where I feel guilty and kind of lose interest in things, they haven’t just been like a couple bad days here and there. Some weeks I’m barely touching dinner, like food just doesn’t appeal to me much, and then other nights I’m up late after everyone’s gone to bed, standing in the kitchen eating crackers or something sweet. That back-and-forth is really what made me feel like something was off enough to come talk about it. Like my book club—I used to pick out the book and think about what I wanted to say, and now even reading the chapters feels like this assignment I’m behind on. I’ll see the book sitting there and just feel tired before I even open it.\n\nAnd cooking used to be something I could get absorbed in, like planning a meal or trying a new recipe, but lately it feels more like another chore in the line of chores. Same with going to galleries. I used to love wandering around and looking at everything slowly, and now I keep putting it off, even when I know I probably would’ve enjoyed it before.\n\nEven the rose beds outside… I used to really care about them. I’d notice every new bud, fuss over them, prune things, check the soil. Now I look at them and think, “I should really get out there,” but it feels like work, so I keep delaying it. It makes me sad, honestly, because those were things that felt like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping differently than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my sleep has definitely been different. There are nights where I’ll only sleep maybe three or four hours, and then I wake up like I’ve had a full night, almost too awake. It’s not that groggy, dragging-myself-out-of-bed feeling. I’ll wake up and feel charged, like my brain is already going before the sun is even up.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking, “Oh, I should write that blog post,” or I’ll start planning some project in my head, like making lists or figuring out steps, and it feels really urgent and doable in the moment. It’s strange because I know three or four hours doesn’t sound like enough sleep, and usually I’d expect to feel awful, but on those mornings I don’t. I feel ready to get up and do things, sometimes before anyone else is even awake.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you only sleep a few hours and feel very energized, do you feel more confident or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it’s not just “I feel a little more optimistic.” It can get bigger than that. During those times, I’ll suddenly feel very sure of myself, like I can see this whole path in front of me and everyone else just hasn’t caught up yet.\n\nOne thing that happens is with this art-therapy workshop idea I’ve had. I haven’t even finished the training yet, so I know, sitting here saying it, that maybe I should be more measured about it. But when I’m in that energized state, I become completely convinced that the workshop is going to change people’s lives in this really major way. Like not just be helpful, but really important. I’ll think, “This is what I’m meant to do, this could reach so many people, this could transform how they heal,” and it feels absolutely true in the moment.\n\nAnd I can get kind of carried away with that feeling of capability, like I’m already almost there even though there are still steps I haven’t done. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because part of me knows I’m still learning, but at the time I feel unusually certain that I can make it happen and that it’s going to be much bigger than maybe it realistically is.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean… I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself talking more in a way that stands out. I’m already someone who can explain things with a lot of detail, as you can probably tell, so it’s a little hard for me to separate “me being me” from something unusual.\n\nDuring those energized times, I might have more thoughts I want to get out, but I can’t say for sure that I’m talking nonstop or that other people have pointed it out to me. I haven’t really been tracking that part. It’s more that my mind feels busy, but whether that turns into me talking more than usual… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it or maybe ask someone who’s around me.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you start taking on more projects or doing more activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually one of the clearer things I notice, once I look back on it. In the moment it just feels like I’m being productive, like I’m finally getting my life organized, but then if I step back it’s a lot all at once.\n\nAt my busiest, I’ll do things like sign up for committees or volunteer for something before I’ve really thought through whether I have the time or energy for it. And then the same day I might be messaging people about workshop ideas, like sending these long messages about art-therapy-type things I want to put together, trying to connect with people or get feedback or make plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll also start several blog drafts, not just one. I’ll have one idea and then another one, and I’ll think, “Oh, this needs to be written too,” and suddenly I have multiple unfinished drafts open or notes everywhere. And then on top of that I’ll start making these big plans for the garden, like what needs to be planted, how it should be laid out, what supplies I need, what I could do differently this year… all in the same day sometimes.\n\nSo yes, I do take on more. It doesn’t always look dramatic from the outside, maybe, but for me it’s like I suddenly have ten tracks running at once and they all feel important and possible right then.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you become more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like my body gets restless in a way that feels different from normal motivation. I don’t just think about doing things — I’m up and moving before I’ve really decided to be.\n\nI’ll start cleaning shelves, like pulling everything off and wiping them down, and then that turns into rearranging my art supplies because suddenly the whole setup feels wrong or inefficient. Then I’ll find a notebook and start drafting poems, or little pieces of writing, and I’ll be moving between that and something else like I can’t stay in one place long enough.\n\nAnd I pace a lot, especially through the garden. I’ll go out there and walk around looking at the plants or the beds, thinking about what should be moved or fixed, and it’s not exactly relaxing. It’s more like… if I stop, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Like there’s this pressure inside me to keep doing something, anything, even if it’s not all connected.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you feel unusually cheerful or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d call it “euphoric” exactly, because that word sounds so extreme, but there are definitely times when I feel unusually bright, like everything is suddenly possible and I can handle anything. It’s almost like I’m lighter, or like I’ve finally clicked into the version of myself I’m supposed to be. I’ll feel very confident, very energized, like, “Okay, now I can fix all of this, now I can do everything.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t always stay cheerful. It can flip really fast. I can go from feeling unstoppable to suddenly furious over something that probably looks small from the outside. Like if my husband moves my paints and I can’t find the exact color or brush I wanted, I’ll get so angry, like he’s ruined the whole flow of what I was doing. Or if he asks about a receipt, just a normal question, I can feel this heat come up in me like he’s accusing me or trying to control me, even if I know later that maybe he wasn’t.\n\nSo yes, there’s that bright, almost overly positive feeling, but it’s mixed with this irritability that can come on really sharply. It’s not just happy. It’s more like everything is turned up too high.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, have they caused problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… they have. I mean, it’s not like everything completely falls apart, but it does create problems, and sometimes I only realize it after I’ve already made things more complicated.\n\nOne big thing is that I’ve fallen behind on my facilitator training. I’ll have all these ideas and intentions, and I’ll start explaining things or planning things in this very expanded way, but then it gets hard to organize it into the actual steps I’m supposed to be doing. So instead of moving forward, I end up with pages of notes or half-plans, and then I’m behind.\n\nThe same thing happens with my private blog work. I’ll have a lot I want to say, but the drafts get scattered, or my explanations get too long and tangled, and then I can’t finish them properly. It’s frustrating because in the moment I feel like I’m doing so much, but the finished work doesn’t match that feeling.\n\nAnd at home, yes, it causes tension with my husband. When I’m in that state, normal routines become harder to keep. Little things turn into discussions or arguments, and then the house rhythm gets thrown off. So it’s mild maybe compared to what it could be, but it definitely affects my training, my writing, and how steady things feel at home.\n\nDoctor: How long do these energized periods usually last?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for… I think about a year and eight months now, if I’m counting it right. Not every single day that whole time, of course, but these energized stretches — where I get overcommitted and wound up and take on too much — they’ve been part of my life for roughly that long.\n\nI didn’t recognize it as a pattern right away. At first I thought I was just having productive phases, or trying to get myself back on track, but looking back, it’s been about a year and eight months that this has been happening in a noticeable way.\n\nDoctor: When one of those energized periods begins, does it usually last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to measure it as exactly four days, because I don’t always mark the start clearly when it’s happening. It’s more like I realize afterward, “Oh, I’ve been in that wound-up, overcommitted mode again.”\n\nWhat I can say is that these stretches have been part of my life for about a year and eight months now. They come and go during that time, but it’s been a noticeable pattern for that long. Sometimes it feels like it lasts long enough that it’s not just one odd day, but I’d have to think carefully to say whether every episode is at least four days in a row.\n\nDoctor: Thinking of one typical energized period, has it ever lasted at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it has felt like it lasts more than just a day or two, but I get a little tangled when I try to pin it down exactly to “four days,” because I don’t always notice the beginning in real time.\n\nWhat I can say is that these energized, overcommitted stretches have been happening as a pattern for about a year and eight months. They come in waves, and when I’m in one, it doesn’t feel like a brief mood for an afternoon — it feels like I’m sort of living in that mode for a stretch. But if you’re asking me to identify one typical one and count the days, I’d say it probably has lasted at least four days at times, even though I don’t always track it neatly.\n\nDoctor: During one of these energized periods, have you ever needed to be hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever been hospitalized specifically because of one of those energized periods. I mean, they can get messy in the sense that I take on too much and feel kind of wound up, and afterward I can look back and think, “Why did I do all that?” But I haven’t had a time where I was admitted to a hospital because of it.\n\nAt least not the way I understand what you’re asking — like needing inpatient care because that energized state got out of control. That hasn’t happened.\n\nDoctor: When you get those gray, heavy periods where you feel down and lose interest, do they usually last at least 2 weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… those gray periods, the heavy ones where I feel guilty and kind of lose interest in things, they haven’t just been like a couple bad days here and there. But if you’re asking me to identify one typical one and count the days, I’d say it probably has lasted at least four days at times, even though I don’t always track it neatly.\n\nDoctor: During one of these energized periods, have you ever needed to be hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever been hospitalized specifically because of one of those energized periods. I mean, they can get messy in the sense that I take on too much and feel kind of wound up, and afterward I can look back and think, “Why did I do all that?” But I haven’t had a time where I was admitted to a hospital because of it.\n\nAt least not the way I understand what you’re asking — like needing inpatient care because that energized state got out of control. That hasn’t happened.\n\nDoctor: When you get those gray, heavy periods where you feel down and lose interest, do they usually last at least 2 weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… those gray periods, the heavy ones where I feel guilty and kind of lose interest in things, they haven’t just been like a couple bad days here and there. I’m not always great at marking the exact start and stop, because sometimes it sort of creeps in and I only realize I’ve been like that after I’ve already been dragging through it.\n\nBut overall, those periods have added up to about seven months. Not all one single block, necessarily, but enough that it feels like a real chunk of time. So when you ask if they last at least two weeks, I’d say yes, they can last that long — it’s more than just a short slump for me."
